I mean, I know they’re built fine—people drive over them all day, trucks and everything—but when I get close, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, I know. I used to hear people complain more about traffic on those bridges than the bridge itself, you know? But for me it’s the height and the water and… yeah, that’s the thing that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other situations where you might be in a high place, like being on the top floor of a building?\n\nPatient: High places in general… I don’t know, I don’t think about them the same way. Like an office building, I can look out a window and maybe feel a little funny if it’s really high, but that’s not the thing.\n\nIt’s really those bridges over the bay. I know they’re built fine and people drive them every day—my coworker takes one like it’s nothing, he complains more about the tolls than the height—but when I get near one, my mind just goes to this picture of the car somehow going right over the side. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges, or does the thought of being a passenger on one of these bridges also cause you significant anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly when I’m the one driving, because I’m right there with my hands on the wheel. But even thinking about those bridges over the bay gets me going a little. I know they’re built fine, people drive over them every day like it’s nothing, but when I get near one, my mind acts like the car could somehow go right over the side.\n\nMy brother-in-law, he’ll drive across and just talk about baseball the whole time, doesn’t even notice. I wish I could be like that, but my head goes straight to the edge of the bridge.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were walking across one of these high bridges, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I mean… walking across one? I don’t really do that, so it’s hard to say exactly. The thing I keep coming back to is those bridges over the bay when I’m in the car. I know they’re built fine, everybody drives over them every day like it’s nothing—people at work complain more about traffic than anything—but when I get near one, my mind starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about the bridge be significantly reduced if someone else were driving you across it?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, if someone else had the wheel… but it doesn’t just disappear. Those bridges over the bay, I know they’re built fine and folks drive them every day—my brother-in-law, like I said, he acts like he’s just going down the block—but when I get near one my mind still goes to, you know, the car somehow going over the side. Even if I’m not driving, that picture can pop in there.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about driving over one of these high bridges when you're not actually in the car cause you anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. If I’m just sitting at home thinking about it, it’s not as bad as actually being there, but it can still get me tense. I know those bridges over the bay are built fine—people drive them every day, probably while eating a sandwich or messing with the radio—but my mind still goes to that picture of the car going over the side. Then I’ll try to think about something else, like work stuff, but it kind of sticks there for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be triggered if you were to drive over a high bridge that doesn't cross water?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still get to me. Maybe the water makes it worse, I don’t know, because I start picturing all that open space, but even if it was just a high bridge over land… as soon as I see it coming up, my chest gets tight. My hands start sweating on the wheel, and I kind of lock my eyes straight ahead like, just get across, just make it to the other side.\n\nIt’s funny, I’ll be fine talking about it before, like if I’m at my desk doing paperwork or dealing with somebody’s benefits forms, and then once the bridge is actually there in front of me, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even had time to talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be less if you were driving over a bridge of the same height, but it was much narrower, so you couldn't see down as much?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah… if I couldn’t see down as much, that might help some. But I don’t think it would take it away. It’s not like I’m sitting there calculating the width of the bridge, you know? I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and everybody drives them every day, but when I get near one my mind still acts like the car could just go right over the side.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about stupid stuff, like guardrails and whether trucks are too close—my wife tells me I grip the wheel like I’m trying to bend it. So narrower might block the view, sure, but I think my head would still know I’m up high on a bridge.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be significantly reduced if you were to drive over a very low bridge, one that doesn't feel high at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a very low one probably wouldn’t bother me much, or maybe not at all. It’s when it feels high, like those big bridges over the bay, that my head starts going places. I know they’re built fine and folks drive over them every day like it’s nothing, but when I get near one my mind acts like the car could go right over the side.\n\nA low bridge… I mean, I drive over little overpasses and stuff all the time and don’t think much of it. Unless traffic’s bad, but that’s a whole different headache.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be the same if you were driving over a high bridge that you know very well, one that you've driven over many times before?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it would be less, because if I’ve driven it a bunch of times I should know it’s fine, right? Like there’s one I’ve had to take enough times for work stuff, and I’ll tell myself, you’ve done this before, nothing happened.\n\nBut honestly, as soon as I see the bridge coming up, it still hits me. My chest gets tight, my hands get sweaty on the wheel, and I just stare straight ahead like, just get to the other side. Familiar or not, once it’s right there in front of me, my body kind of does what it does.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be present if you were not driving, but rather just thinking about someone else driving over one?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s not the same as when I’m the one driving up to it. If I’m just picturing someone else doing it, my mind can still go there—like, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine, people use them every day, but I start imagining the car going over the side anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, my cousin drives over one of those like it’s nothing, talks on the phone about groceries and all that—well, hands-free, I guess—but me, even thinking about it can make me uneasy. Not full-on like being there, but yeah, the thought can bother me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be present if you were a passenger in a car being driven by someone else over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still be there. Maybe not quite the same as when I’m the one holding the wheel, but if we’re coming up on one of those high bridges over the bay, I still get that feeling.\n\nI know, logically, they’re built fine. People drive them every day like it’s nothing—my brother-in-law will cross one and complain about the tolls more than the height. But my mind still goes to, you know, what if the car somehow goes over the side. Even if someone else is driving, being in the car near that bridge can set that off.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be completely absent if you were to avoid driving over them altogether, but still had to be near or under them for some other reason?\n\nPatient: If I’m just near one, like not having to drive over it, it’s not really the same. The big thing for me is the driving over it. That’s what I’ve been doing—planning my whole day sometimes around not having to take those high bridges.\n\nLike, I’ll leave earlier or take some ridiculous route, even if it adds time, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I work in human resources, I’m supposed to be the organized one, you know? I’ll tell people it’s traffic or construction, because there’s always construction somewhere.\n\nBut if I can avoid actually driving over it, it’s a lot better. If I can’t avoid it, then I just kind of grip the wheel and push through it the whole way. So near or under it, maybe I’d notice it, but it’s crossing it that really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be present if you were to ride a bicycle over one of these bridges?\n\nPatient: On a bicycle? I mean, I don’t really picture myself doing that much, but yeah, if it was one of those high bridges over the bay, I think it’d still get to me.\n\nI know they’re built fine—people go over them every day, cars, trucks, probably cyclists too, and everybody acts like it’s normal. But when I get near one, my mind starts doing that thing where it’s like, what if you go right over the side. Even saying bicycle makes it feel worse in a way, because you’re more exposed, you know?\n\nI used to ride bikes more when I was younger, before work and family and all that. Now it’s mostly sitting at a desk, HR paperwork, birthdays in the office, people arguing over schedules—but yeah, the bridge part would still be the problem.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be present if you were to cross a high bridge on foot, without any vehicle?\n\nPatient: On foot? Yeah, I think it would still be there. Maybe even… I don’t know, maybe worse in some ways, because there’s no car around you.\n\nI know those bridges over the bay are built fine. People go over them every day like it’s nothing. I see folks driving, sometimes walking near those areas, and they’re just going about their day. But when I get near one, my mind starts doing that thing where it’s like, what if you go over the side. Usually I picture it with the car, honestly, the car somehow going right over, but being on foot up there would still set that off.\n\nIt’s funny, because I can handle plenty of things at work—people coming into HR all upset about schedules or paychecks—but then a bridge just kind of gets in my head.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be significantly reduced if you were to cross one in a vehicle that you are not driving, but still have a clear view of the height?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little reduced if someone else was driving, because I wouldn’t be the one steering, but no, it wouldn’t go away. If I can see how high up we are, that’s still what gets me.\n\nI know those bridges over the bay are built fine, and people drive them every single day like it’s nothing. I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this is normal, this is safe… and then my mind goes, what if the car goes right over the side. Even as a passenger, I think I’d still be watching the edge too much.\n\nIt’s like when my wife drives sometimes and I’m supposed to just relax, but then I notice every little thing—though she says I notice the wrong things, like I can tell you where every gas station is but forget what we went to the store for. But with the bridge, yeah, having a clear view of the height would still make me anxious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Even as a passenger, I think I’d still be watching the edge too much.\n\nIt’s like when my wife drives sometimes and I’m supposed to just relax, but then I notice every little thing—though she says I notice the wrong things, like I can tell you where every gas station is but forget what we went to the store for. But with the bridge, yeah, having a clear view of the height would still make me anxious.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould your anxiety about high bridges be the same if you were to drive over one in a vehicle with a completely enclosed cabin, such as a bus, where you couldn't see the height outside?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If I really couldn’t see the height at all, like in a bus with no view out or something, I think it might be less. The seeing over the side is a big part of what sets my head off.

But even then, if I knew we were on one of those high bridges over the bay, I don’t know that I’d be totally calm. I know those bridges are built fine, and people drive over them every day like it’s nothing. But when I get near one, my mind starts acting like the car could just go right over the side.

It’s kind of like how at work I can know a policy is straightforward, but somebody comes in upset and suddenly everything feels bigger than it is. With the bridge, even if I can’t see much, just knowing where I am might still make me tense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the main thing is driving. Not all driving, exactly—like I can go to work, run errands, that kind of thing—but I keep planning my whole day around avoiding those really high bridges. I’ll take these ridiculous longer routes, and then I’m late and annoyed, and it reminds me of how my dad used to say, “Leave ten minutes early,” which I never did.\n\nBut, yeah… if I can’t get around a bridge, I grip the wheel and just sort of force myself through it the whole way. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess it does come on pretty fast. Like as soon as I see the bridge coming up, my chest gets tight and my hands start sweating on the wheel. I kind of lock my eyes straight ahead, like if I just don’t look around and make it to the other side, I’ll be fine.\n\nIt’s funny, I’ll be thinking about something completely normal before that—work stuff, or whether I forgot to pick up milk—and then the bridge is there and it’s like my body just flips a switch.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of fear or discomfort in situations other than driving over high bridges?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way. It’s mostly those high bridges, the ones over the bay especially. I mean, I know they’re built fine—people drive over them all day, trucks and everything—but when I get close, my head starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, I know. I used to hear people complain more about traffic on those bridges than the bridge itself, you know? But for me it’s the height and the water and… yeah, that’s the thing that gets me.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other situations where you might be in a high place, like being on the top floor of a building?\n\nPatient: High places in general… I don’t know, I don’t think about them the same way. Like an office building, I can look out a window and maybe feel a little funny if it’s really high, but that’s not the thing.\n\nIt’s really those bridges over the bay. I know they’re built fine and people drive them every day—my coworker takes one like it’s nothing, he complains more about the tolls than the height—but when I get near one, my mind just goes to this picture of the car somehow going right over the side. That’s the part I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges, or does the thought of being a passenger on one of these bridges also cause you significant anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly when I’m the one driving, because I’m right there with my hands on the wheel. But even thinking about those bridges over the bay gets me going a little. I know they’re built fine, people drive over them every day like it’s nothing, but when I get near one, my mind acts like the car could somehow go right over the side.\n\nMy brother-in-law, he’ll drive across and just talk about baseball the whole time, doesn’t even notice. I wish I could be like that, but my head goes straight to the edge of the bridge.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you were walking across one of these high bridges, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I mean… walking across one? I don’t really do that, so it’s hard to say exactly. The thing I keep coming back to is those bridges over the bay when I’m in the car. I know they’re built fine, everybody drives over them every day like it’s nothing—people at work complain more about traffic than anything—but when I get near one, my mind starts acting like the car could somehow go right over the side.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about the bridge be significantly reduced if someone else were driving you across it?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, if someone else had the wheel… but it doesn’t just disappear. Those bridges over the bay, I know they’re built fine and folks drive them every day—my brother-in-law, like I said, he acts like he’s just going down the block—but when I get near one my mind still goes to, you know, the car somehow going over the side. Even if I’m not driving, that picture can pop in there.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about driving over one of these high bridges when you're not actually in the car cause you anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. If I’m just sitting at home thinking about it, it’s not as bad as actually being there, but it can still get me tense. I know those bridges over the bay are built fine—people drive them every day, probably while eating a sandwich or messing with the radio—but my mind still goes to that picture of the car going over the side. Then I’ll try to think about something else, like work stuff, but it kind of sticks there for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be triggered if you were to drive over a high bridge that doesn't cross water?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still get to me. Maybe the water makes it worse, I don’t know, because I start picturing all that open space, but even if it was just a high bridge over land… as soon as I see it coming up, my chest gets tight. My hands start sweating on the wheel, and I kind of lock my eyes straight ahead like, just get across, just make it to the other side.\n\nIt’s funny, I’ll be fine talking about it before, like if I’m at my desk doing paperwork or dealing with somebody’s benefits forms, and then once the bridge is actually there in front of me, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even had time to talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be less if you were driving over a bridge of the same height, but it was much narrower, so you couldn't see down as much?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah… if I couldn’t see down as much, that might help some. But I don’t think it would take it away. It’s not like I’m sitting there calculating the width of the bridge, you know? I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and everybody drives them every day, but when I get near one my mind still acts like the car could just go right over the side.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about stupid stuff, like guardrails and whether trucks are too close—my wife tells me I grip the wheel like I’m trying to bend it. So narrower might block the view, sure, but I think my head would still know I’m up high on a bridge.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be significantly reduced if you were to drive over a very low bridge, one that doesn't feel high at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a very low one probably wouldn’t bother me much, or maybe not at all. It’s when it feels high, like those big bridges over the bay, that my head starts going places. I know they’re built fine and folks drive over them every day like it’s nothing, but when I get near one my mind acts like the car could go right over the side.\n\nA low bridge… I mean, I drive over little overpasses and stuff all the time and don’t think much of it. Unless traffic’s bad, but that’s a whole different headache.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be the same if you were driving over a high bridge that you know very well, one that you've driven over many times before?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it would be less, because if I’ve driven it a bunch of times I should know it’s fine, right? Like there’s one I’ve had to take enough times for work stuff, and I’ll tell myself, you’ve done this before, nothing happened.\n\nBut honestly, as soon as I see the bridge coming up, it still hits me. My chest gets tight, my hands get sweaty on the wheel, and I just stare straight ahead like, just get to the other side. Familiar or not, once it’s right there in front of me, my body kind of does what it does.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be present if you were not driving, but rather just thinking about someone else driving over one?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s not the same as when I’m the one driving up to it. If I’m just picturing someone else doing it, my mind can still go there—like, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine, people use them every day, but I start imagining the car going over the side anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, my cousin drives over one of those like it’s nothing, talks on the phone about groceries and all that—well, hands-free, I guess—but me, even thinking about it can make me uneasy. Not full-on like being there, but yeah, the thought can bother me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be present if you were a passenger in a car being driven by someone else over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still be there. Maybe not quite the same as when I’m the one holding the wheel, but if we’re coming up on one of those high bridges over the bay, I still get that feeling.\n\nI know, logically, they’re built fine. People drive them every day like it’s nothing—my brother-in-law will cross one and complain about the tolls more than the height. But my mind still goes to, you know, what if the car somehow goes over the side. Even if someone else is driving, being in the car near that bridge can set that off.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be completely absent if you were to avoid driving over them altogether, but still had to be near or under them for some other reason?\n\nPatient: If I’m just near one, like not having to drive over it, it’s not really the same. The big thing for me is the driving over it. That’s what I’ve been doing—planning my whole day sometimes around not having to take those high bridges.\n\nLike, I’ll leave earlier or take some ridiculous route, even if it adds time, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I work in human resources, I’m supposed to be the organized one, you know? I’ll tell people it’s traffic or construction, because there’s always construction somewhere.\n\nBut if I can avoid actually driving over it, it’s a lot better. If I can’t avoid it, then I just kind of grip the wheel and push through it the whole way. So near or under it, maybe I’d notice it, but it’s crossing it that really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be present if you were to ride a bicycle over one of these bridges?\n\nPatient: On a bicycle? I mean, I don’t really picture myself doing that much, but yeah, if it was one of those high bridges over the bay, I think it’d still get to me.\n\nI know they’re built fine—people go over them every day, cars, trucks, probably cyclists too, and everybody acts like it’s normal. But when I get near one, my mind starts doing that thing where it’s like, what if you go right over the side. If I’m just sitting at home thinking about it, it’s not as bad as actually being there, but it can still get me tense. I know those bridges over the bay are built fine—people drive them every day, probably while eating a sandwich or messing with the radio—but my mind still goes to that picture of the car going over the side. Then I’ll try to think about something else, like work stuff, but it kind of sticks there for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be triggered if you were to drive over a high bridge that doesn't cross water?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still get to me. Maybe the water makes it worse, I don’t know, because I start picturing all that open space, but even if it was just a high bridge over land… as soon as I see it coming up, my chest gets tight. My hands start sweating on the wheel, and I kind of lock my eyes straight ahead like, just get across, just make it to the other side.\n\nIt’s funny, I’ll be fine talking about it before, like if I’m at my desk doing paperwork or dealing with somebody’s benefits forms, and then once the bridge is actually there in front of me, it’s like my body reacts before I’ve even had time to talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be less if you were driving over a bridge of the same height, but it was much narrower, so you couldn't see down as much?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah… if I couldn’t see down as much, that might help some. But I don’t think it would take it away. It’s not like I’m sitting there calculating the width of the bridge, you know? I know those bridges over the bay are built fine and everybody drives them every day, but when I get near one my mind still acts like the car could just go right over the side.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about stupid stuff, like guardrails and whether trucks are too close—my wife tells me I grip the wheel like I’m trying to bend it. So narrower might block the view, sure, but I think my head would still know I’m up high on a bridge.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be significantly reduced if you were to drive over a very low bridge, one that doesn't feel high at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a very low one probably wouldn’t bother me much, or maybe not at all. It’s when it feels high, like those big bridges over the bay, that my head starts going places. I know they’re built fine and folks drive over them every day like it’s nothing, but when I get near one my mind acts like the car could go right over the side.\n\nA low bridge… I mean, I drive over little overpasses and stuff all the time and don’t think much of it. Unless traffic’s bad, but that’s a whole different headache.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be the same if you were driving over a high bridge that you know very well, one that you've driven over many times before?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it would be less, because if I’ve driven it a bunch of times I should know it’s fine, right? Like there’s one I’ve had to take enough times for work stuff, and I’ll tell myself, you’ve done this before, nothing happened.\n\nBut honestly, as soon as I see the bridge coming up, it still hits me. My chest gets tight, my hands get sweaty on the wheel, and I just stare straight ahead like, just get to the other side. Familiar or not, once it’s right there in front of me, my body kind of does what it does.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be present if you were not driving, but rather just thinking about someone else driving over one?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s not the same as when I’m the one driving up to it. If I’m just picturing someone else doing it, my mind can still go there—like, I know those bridges over the bay are built fine, people use them every day, but I start imagining the car going over the side anyway.\n\nIt’s funny, my cousin drives over one of those like it’s nothing, talks on the phone about groceries and all that—well, hands-free, I guess—but me, even thinking about it can make me uneasy. Not full-on like being there, but yeah, the thought can bother me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be present if you were a passenger in a car being driven by someone else over a high bridge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would still be there. Maybe not quite the same as when I’m the one holding the wheel, but if we’re coming up on one of those high bridges over the bay, I still get that feeling.\n\nI know, logically, they’re built fine. People drive them every day like it’s nothing—my brother-in-law will cross one and complain about the tolls more than the height. But my mind still goes to, you know, what if the car somehow goes over the side. Even if someone else is driving, being in the car near that bridge can set that off.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be completely absent if you were to avoid driving over them altogether, but still had to be near or under them for some other reason?\n\nPatient: If I’m just near one, like not having to drive over it, it’s not really the same. The big thing for me is the driving over it. That’s what I’ve been doing—planning my whole day sometimes around not having to take those high bridges.\n\nLike, I’ll leave earlier or take some ridiculous route, even if it adds time, and then I’m annoyed at myself because I work in human resources, I’m supposed to be the organized one, you know? I’ll tell people it’s traffic or construction, because there’s always construction somewhere.\n\nBut if I can avoid actually driving over it, it’s a lot better. If I can’t avoid it, then I just kind of grip the wheel and push through it the whole way. So near or under it, maybe I’d notice it, but it’s crossing it that really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be present if you were to ride a bicycle over one of these bridges?\n\nPatient: On a bicycle? I mean, I don’t really picture myself doing that much, but yeah, if it was one of those high bridges over the bay, I think it’d still get to me.\n\nI know they’re built fine—people go over them every day, cars, trucks, probably cyclists too, and everybody acts like it’s normal. But when I get near one, my mind starts doing that thing where it’s like, what if you go right over the side. Even saying bicycle makes it feel worse in a way, because you’re more exposed, you know?\n\nI used to ride bikes more when I was younger, before work and family and all that. Now it’s mostly sitting at a desk, HR paperwork, birthdays in the office, people arguing over schedules—but yeah, the bridge part would still be the problem.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety be present if you were to cross a high bridge on foot, without any vehicle?\n\nPatient: On foot? Yeah, I think it would still be there. Maybe even… I don’t know, maybe worse in some ways, because there’s no car around you.\n\nI know those bridges over the bay are built fine. People go over them every day like it’s nothing. I see folks driving, sometimes walking near those areas, and they’re just going about their day. But when I get near one, my mind starts doing that thing where it’s like, what if you go over the side. Usually I picture it with the car, honestly, the car somehow going right over, but being on foot up there would still set that off.\n\nIt’s funny, because I can handle plenty of things at work—people coming into HR all upset about schedules or paychecks—but then a bridge just kind of gets in my head.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be significantly reduced if you were to cross one in a vehicle that you are not driving, but still have a clear view of the height?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little reduced if someone else was driving, because I wouldn’t be the one steering, but no, it wouldn’t go away. If I can see how high up we are, that’s still what gets me.\n\nI know those bridges over the bay are built fine, and people drive them every single day like it’s nothing. I’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, this is normal, this is safe… and then my mind goes, what if the car goes right over the side. Even as a passenger, I think I’d still be watching the edge too much.\n\nIt’s like when my wife drives sometimes and I’m supposed to just relax, but then I notice every little thing—though she says I notice the wrong things, like I can tell you where every gas station is but forget what we went to the store for. But with the bridge, yeah, having a clear view of the height would still make me anxious.\n\nDoctor: Would your anxiety about high bridges be the same if you were to drive over one in a vehicle with a completely enclosed cabin, such as a bus, where you couldn't see the height outside?\n\nPatient: If I really couldn’t see the height at all, like in a bus with no view out or something, I think it might be less. The seeing over the side is a big part of what sets my head off.\n\nBut even then, if I knew we were on one of those high bridges over the bay, I don’t know that I’d be totally calm. I know those bridges are built fine, and people drive over them every day like it’s nothing. But when I get near one, my mind starts acting like the car could just go right over the side.\n\nIt’s kind of like how at work I can know a policy is straightforward, but somebody comes in upset and suddenly everything feels bigger than it is. With the bridge, even if I can’t see much, just knowing where I am might still make me tense."
  }
